# TESTIMONIES OF THE DOCTORS OF THE LAW,
## *proving the chemical art to be lawful.*

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'O' containing a personified face (likely a sun) with rays, framed within a square border.</image-desc>OLDRADUS DE PONTE <note>Oldradus was an influential 14th-century Italian jurist whose legal opinions, or "consilia," were cited for centuries.</note> in his legal opinion number 74, which begins "Whether an Alchemist," <note>original: "An Alchymista"</note> asserts: That alchemists, who produce silver or gold from tin, lead, or another base metal, are not to be condemned, but rather should be pursued with praise, provided they do not do this through the magical arts or any other art hateful to the laws. Concerning these forbidden arts, it is held in Law 1 of the Code <term>On Treasures</term>, book 10. <note>This refers to the *Codex Justinianus*, a foundation of Roman Law.</note> For just as those who are engaged in the investigation of metals are decorated with many privileges by the lawmakers—as seen in the entire title of the Code <term>On Metals</term>, book 11—so also the laws pursue Alchemy with great favor. This is because it is an imitator of nature itself and because of the public utility <note>The "public utility" refers to the potential for alchemy to create wealth and useful materials for the state.</note> that seems to result from it.

   Nor should this seem unfitting, since we frequently see living things being generated from dead things. ...to make silver from tin. For since they are, as it were, from the same source and from the same principle, there is an easier transition between them, as they possess the same "symbol" <note>original: "symbolum." In Aristotelian and alchemical thought, this refers to shared qualities or "elements" that make it possible for one substance to change into another.</note>. This is supported by the argument in § 1 of the *Institutes*, in the section "By what means an obligation is contracted," and similar legal passages. <note>The author is using a legal analogy from Roman Law to explain why physical transformation is possible.</note>

For there are within physical things, throughout all the primary elements, certain hidden and **seminal reasons** <term>seminal reasons</term> <note>Latin: *seminariae rationes*. When the right temporal and causal opportunity is provided, these burst forth into their proper species in their own ways and times, as described in the text of Augustine’s *City of God*, in the chapter "No wonder," Question 26, point 5.

These are the words of Master **Oldendorp** <note>Johann Oldendorp (c. 1487–1567), a prominent German jurist. The text here makes a curious chronological claim about who quoted whom.</note>, whom **Johannes Andreae** <note>A famous 14th-century legal scholar.</note>—in his additions to the *Speculum* <note>The *Speculum Iudiciale*, a standard medieval legal textbook.</note> in the book concerning the crime of forgery—recites word-for-word, passing them off as his own, as is his habit. Because he was also a great alchemist, he created certain **golden rods**, which he allowed to be subjected to every test of purity.

# SEVEN TREATISES OR

## The Chapters of Hermes Trismegistus, the Golden.

In the histories of divine matters, we read of three illustrious philosophers, each of whom was named Hermes. The first of these was **Enoch** <note>In Hermetic tradition, the biblical patriarch Enoch is often identified with the Egyptian god Thoth or Hermes, credited with bringing hidden knowledge to humanity.</note> before the Great Flood, who departed for heaven in a chariot of fire, accompanied by angels. The second was **Noah**, who, by God's command, escaped safely in the ark from the flood of many waters. For each of them was called by the name Hermes in one place, and by the name Mercury <note>The Roman name for Hermes; here the author emphasizes the syncretism of biblical and pagan figures.</note> in another, to distinguish them from the Hermes who reigned in Egypt after the flood, whom we have introduced here. 

For this third Hermes was a most famous man, who, adorned with a royal crown, ruled as king over Egypt for a long time. He was called "thrice-great" <note>original: "ter magnus" or "Trismegistus." This title usually refers to his mastery over the three parts of the wisdom of the whole universe: alchemy, astrology, and theurgy.</note> because of his threefold virtue. For they say that he was a **King**, a **Philosopher**, and a **Prophet**, and he is also said to have been the inventor of all liberal and mechanical disciplines. **Geber** the Philosopher <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–c. 815), the most influential figure in early Islamic chemistry, whose works were later translated into Latin and became foundational for European alchemy.</note>, who was a King of the Persians, [calls] him the prince...

But unless I feared the day of judgment, or the damnation of my soul for hiding this science, I would reveal nothing of this science, nor would I prophesy to anyone. Truly, I wished to render what is due to the faithful, just as the Author of Faith <note>A reference to God as the source of all truth and belief.</note> has deigned to bestow it upon me.

Understand, sons of the wise—you who follow the ancient Philosophers—the science of the four elements <note>The four elements are Earth, Air, Fire, and Water, which alchemists believed were the building blocks of all matter.</note> not in a physical or imprudent manner; these elements are enduring according to their own principles and their hidden operation. For their operation is indeed secret, because nothing acts unless it is composed, and it is not perfected unless its colors are first brought to completion. <note>The "colors" refer to the specific stages of the alchemical Great Work, traditionally progressing from black (decay), to white (purification), to yellow (perfection), and finally to red (the Philosopher's Stone).</note>

Know, sons of the wise, that there is a division of the water used by the ancient Philosophers, which divides itself into four others: one to two, and three to one. The third part of these is for the color—namely, the moisture of the one coagulating it <note>Coagulation is the process of turning a liquid substance into a solid or semi-solid state.</note>—while two-thirds of the water are the **weights of the wise**. <term>weights of the wise</term><note>Refers to the precise, secret proportions of ingredients required for a successful alchemical reaction.</note>

# CHEMICA.

...[tem]perately through half an ounce, and of orpiment <term>Orpiment (original: Auripigmentum); a deep yellow arsenic sulfide mineral used as a pigment and in alchemical recipes to simulate the color of gold.</term> a half, which are eight—that is, three ounces. Know that the "vine of the wise" <note>original: "vitis sapientum." A common alchemical metaphor for the material from which the "wine" or transformative spirit is extracted.</note> is extracted in three, and its wine is completed at the end of thirty. Understand therefore the operation: the boiling diminishes it, but the tincture <note>A "tincture" in alchemy is a potent substance—often the goal of the work—capable of infusing and transforming the properties and colors of other metals.</note> increases it, just as the Moon <note>original: "Luna." Alchemists associated the Moon with the metal silver and the "white" stage of the Great Work; its cycles of waxing and waning represent the growth and reduction of the material during processing.</note> is diminished after fifteen days and increased on the third. This, therefore, is the beginning and the end.

Behold, I have explained to you what had been hidden, since the work is with you and among you; by taking it from within, you can possess that which remains in the earth or in the sea. Guard, therefore, the quicksilver <term>Quicksilver (original: argentum vivum); the metal mercury, considered by alchemists to be the "spirit" of all metals and a primary ingredient in the Philosopher’s Stone.</term>, which is created in the innermost chambers where it has been coagulated. For it is the quicksilver itself which is spoken of as the "residual earth."

He who now hears my words, let him seek from Him <note>A reference to God as the ultimate judge of the alchemist's character and intentions.</note> who justifies the work of no evildoer and deprives no benefactor of rewards. Because I have uncovered everything that had been hidden concerning this science, I have revealed the greatest secret <term>Arcanum; a term for a deep mystery or a hidden medicinal/alchemical secret.</term> and knowledge to those who possess understanding.

Know then, seekers of reports and sons of wisdom, that the vulture upon the mountain... <note>The "vulture" is a classic alchemical symbol often representing the process of sublimation—where a substance is heated until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again, "flying" up the neck of the flask like a bird to the mountain top.</note>

# ON THE ART

...[tem]perately existing, it cries out with a loud voice: I am the white of the black, and the red of the white, and the yellow of the red, and certainly I am truthful. And know that the head of the art is the raven <note>original: "coruus." In alchemy, the raven represents the *Nigredo* or "blackening" stage, where the matter is decomposed and purified through "death."</note>, which flies in the darkness of night and the clarity of day, without wings. From the bitterness existing in its throat, the coloring is obtained; from its true body, redness goes forth; and from its back, pure water is taken.

Understand therefore and receive the gift of God, and hide it from all the foolish. It is hidden within the caverns of the metals, this which is the venerable stone <term>Venerable stone (original: lapis uenerabilis); a name for the Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the Great Work, which was believed to possess a "sublime mind" or spirit.</term>, splendid in color, <margin>now</margin> a sublime mind, and an open sea. Behold, I have explained it to you. Give thanks to God who taught you this science, for He loves the grateful.

*(A hand with a pointing finger in the margin indicates the next paragraph.)*

Therefore, place it in a moist fire <term>Moist fire (original: humido igne); a method of heating, such as a water bath or "Bain-marie," which provides gentle, indirect heat through steam or water rather than direct flame.</term> and cause it to cook, which increases the heat of the moisture and kills the dryness of incombustibility <note>original: "incombustionis siccitatem." This refers to the chemical property of a substance that refuses to burn or change; the alchemist seeks to "kill" this resistance to allow transformation.</note>, until the root appears; then extract the redness and the light part from it, until a third part remains.

Sons of the wise, the Philosophers have been called envious for this reason—not because they envy the well-mannered, nor the religious, nor the lawful,

### CHEMISTRY.

...[wicked] men, or those who are wise but ignorant, corrupt, and lacking in law and kindness, lest the evil become powerful to commit sins, and for which the **Philosophers** would have to give an account to God; for all the wicked are unworthy of wisdom.

Know that I call this stone by its name, for they have called it the **woman of magnesia** <note>original: "foeminam magnefiæ." In alchemy, "magnesia" does not refer to the modern element magnesium, but rather to a primary, "magnetic" mineral substance or the "mother" material of the Stone.</note>, or the **hen**, or even **white saliva**, **milk of the birds** <note>original: "lac uolatiliũ." A poetic term for the volatile, white spirit or distilled essence released during the heating of the matter.</note>, and **unburnt ash**; they do this so that they might hide it from the foolish who lack intellect, law, and kindness. Yet, I have named it for the wise by its one well-known name: the **Philosopher's Stone** <term>Philosopher's Stone (original: lapis sapientum); the legendary goal of the "Great Work," believed to be a substance that could transmute base metals into gold and act as a universal medicine.</term>. Preserve, therefore, within it the sea, the fire, and the **winged spirit of heaven** <note>original: "coeli uolatile." This likely refers to a volatile substance that "flies" up as vapor when heated.</note>, until the moment of its emergence. 

I beseech you all, **sons of the Philosophers** <note>original: "filios Philofophorũ." A traditional address to students of alchemy, implying a spiritual or intellectual lineage rather than biological descent.</note>, by our benefactor who grants you the beauty of His grace, that you reveal the name of this stone to no fool, ignorant person, or anyone unfit. No one has granted me anything for which I have not returned what he gave, nor have I ceased to honor him, and I have placed in this the best interpretation. My son, this stone is hidden, of many colors,

# ON THE ART

...and [having been] joined in one color, know it and keep it secret. With the Almighty’s permission, you will escape from the greatest sickness, sorrow, every harm, and distress; from darkness to light, from the desert to a dwelling place, and from narrowness to expansion <note>original: "de anguſtia ad dilatationem." This refers to a transition from the 'tightness' of labor or spiritual trial to the 'breadth' of success and divine favor.</note>, you shall come by His help.

### CHAPTER II.

My son, before all else I advise you to **fear God**, in whom lies the strength of your undertaking <note>original: "diſpoſitionis." In an alchemical context, the 'disposition' refers to the specific arrangement or preparation of the experiment.</note> and the unification of all things set apart. Son, whatever you hear, reason through it, for I do not consider you to be without reason or foolish. Therefore, receive my encouragements and meditate on them; prepare your heart as if you were the very author of these warnings. For him whose nature is hot, if he becomes cold, it will not harm him; similarly, he who is rational should close the door of ignorance against himself, lest he be deceived while feeling secure.

Son, take the **volatile** <term>volatile (original: uolatile); a substance that easily evaporates or escapes as vapor when heated, often symbolized by birds or wings.</term>, submerge the flying thing, and separate it from its **rust** <note>original: "rubigine." This term refers to the impurities, oxidation, or 'dross' that must be removed to reveal the pure matter within.</note> which destroys it; extract and cast it away from the thing, so that it may become alive and answer to you—not by flying away into distant regions, but flying openly

# ALCHEMY

...it might contain. For if you have extracted it from narrowness after narrowness <note>original: "ab angustia post angustiam." Son, take this redness corrupted by water—just as fire is a bearer—a living ash <note>original: "cinis uiuus." This often refers to quicklime or a highly reactive, "living" residue left after calcination.</note>. If you always remove this from it until the redness is purified for you, a fellowship is formed for you, in which it grows warm and in which it rests.

Son, return the charcoal, whose life is extinguished, to the water for thirty days known to you; then you are a crowned king, resting above the well, away from that <term>orpiment (original: Auripigmento); a deep orange-yellow arsenic sulfide mineral, used as a pigment and in alchemy to represent the "yellowing" stage or sulfur.</term>, which lacks moisture. Now I have made the hearts of those listening rejoice—those who hope in you—and your own eyes looking at you, in hope of that which you contain.

Son, let it be known to you that water was first in the air, then in the earth; you also must return it to the higher regions. Through its own channels, change it not unskillfully; then...

# CHEMICAL MATTERS

I bestow delights upon those who understand, and I make them recognize and understand what they seek, and to possess divine things. In alchemy, the Sun represents Gold or the fixed, masculine principle of the Work.</note> follows, wishing nonetheless to rule, to guard the art, and to join the son of the water <note>original: "aquae filium." Listeners, understand! And henceforth let us use our own judgments; and those things which I have written, I have demonstrated to you through the most subtle investigation and contemplation. I have known a certain "One" <note>This refers to the "Unum Re" or the "One Thing" from which the entire Work proceeds, emphasizing the unity of the material.</note>; for who understands what is sought by sincere investigation and reason? From a man comes nothing but what is like him, and from a bull comes the same. And if any animal mates with a foreign species, that which is born is like neither. <note>This is a foundational alchemical principle: to make gold, one must start with the "seed" of gold. Nature only produces like from like; hybrid or "foreign" mixtures are seen as sterile or monstrous.</note>

Therefore, to me and my brother..."

# ON THE ART

...joined to mine, nothing is better and more venerable. Moreover, the King <note>original: "Rex." In alchemical allegory, the King represents the completed Philosopher's Stone or the perfected Gold of the Sages.</note> rules, as his brothers bear witness, saying:

> I am crowned and adorned with a diadem, and I am clothed in your kingdom, and I pour joy into hearts; and I, bound in the arms and to the breast of my mother and her substance, cause my substance to be contained and to rest, and I compose the invisible from the visible; then the hidden shall appear, and everything that the Philosophers have hidden shall be generated from us.

O listeners, understand these words, guard them, meditate upon them, and seek nothing else. Man is generated by nature from the beginning, whose innards are fleshy, and not from anything else. Meditate on these letters, but reject the superfluous. Whence the Philosopher says:

> <term>Both</term> <note>original: "Both." This is likely a technical term for a specific alchemical preparation, perhaps a corruption of "Borith" (a cleansing agent) or an Arabic term for a mineral salt.</note> is made from the yellow <note>original: "citrino." In the sequence of the alchemical "Great Work," the yellow stage (citrinitas) follows the white and represents a transition toward the final red stage.</note> which is extracted from the red knot, and from nothing else; for if it becomes yellow, your wisdom will be achieved; let it not concern you if you do not strive to extract it from the red.

And guard it more diligently, lest it should exit from the vessel. And know that the "seasons" of the earth are found in the water, and let it remain so until you place the earth itself upon it. Therefore, once it has been liquefied and burnt, take its **brain** <term>cerebrum</term> <note>original: "cerebrum." In alchemical texts, the "brain" often refers to the white, volatile essence or the soft, uppermost part of the matter after a successful distillation.</note> and grind it with the sharpest vinegar, or with the **urine of boys** <note>original: "urina puerorum." When this is completed, it lives in **putrefaction** <note>original: "putrefactione." The essential stage of decay where the material "dies" to its old form so it can be reborn in a higher state.</note>; the dark clouds that were in it before it died in its body will be transformed. However, having been repeated exactly as I described, it dies again, and as I said, it lives from there. In its life and in its death, we make use of the **spirits** <note>original: "spiritibus." These are the volatile components or gases released during heating that must be captured and reunited with the "body" or solid residue.</note>. For just as it dies when the spirits are taken away, so it is brought to life when they are restored, and it rejoices in them. 

Upon reaching this point, you will see with certainty that which you seek. I also tell you the signs of **rejoicing** <note>original: "lætificationis." Fixation is the process of making a volatile substance stable so that it no longer evaporates when exposed to fire.</note> its own body. By this configuration, just as your predecessors reached their goal—behold, they are now dead. Now I have demonstrated the goal to you and opened the book to the skilled; I have hidden the secret things, caused the separated things to be contained, joined various shapes, and associated the spirits. Receive this gift from God.

## CHAPTER VI.

You ought to give thanks to God, who bestows wisdom itself upon every wise person, which frees us from misery and poverty. With all its substances, I am about to attempt probable miracles and humbly pray to God, so that while we live we may reach Him. From this point, **Sons of the Philosophers** <note>original: "filij Philosophorum." A traditional term for students or initiates into the alchemical arts.</note>, the **ointments** <note>original: "unguenta." Literally "green of copper," a green pigment or crust formed on copper, often used as a reagent.</note>, **tragacanth** <note>original: "tragacantho." A natural gum obtained from the dried sap of several species of Middle Eastern legumes.</note>, and bones, are written in the books of the ancients.

Furthermore, we must set forth the **arrangement** <term>dispositio</term> <note>The specific preparation, setup, or internal order of the material.</note> of the ointment that contains **tinctures** <note>The concentrated colors or essences that have the power to transform other bodies.</note>, which coagulates fleeting things and beautifies sulfurs; we must prefer this to all other perfected ointments. For we know the preciousness and essence of its vessel, which is called **Divine Sulfur**, and its form for other ointments. It is an ointment that is hidden and buried, whose arrangement cannot be seen, and it lives in its body just as fire lives in trees and stones; we must extract this with the most subtle art and skill, without **burning** <note>original: "combustione." The text ends here mid-word, completed by the catchword "stione" on the next page.</note>—

### CHAPTER VII.

O Sons <note>A traditional opening address in alchemical texts, referring to the "Sons of the Art" or students of alchemy.</note>, the <term>bodies of the Philosophers</term> <note>original: "Philosophorũ corpora." In alchemical theory, these are the seven metals associated with the celestial bodies: gold, silver, mercury, copper, iron, tin, and lead.</note> are seven, of which the first is their most perfect gold, the king, and the head. Neither the earth corrupts it, nor do burning things <note>Meaning fire or caustic substances.</note> devastate it, nor does water alter it, because its constitution is tempered and its nature is balanced in heat, cold, and moisture; nor is there anything superfluous in it. Therefore, the Philosophers have preferred and magnified it, saying that gold is held among the bodies just as the Sun is more splendid among the stars by its own light; for by it, by the will of God, every vegetable and every fruit of the earth is perfected. Thus, gold contains and vivifies every body, because it is the <term>Ixsir</term> <note>original: "Ixsir." An Arabic-derived term for the Elixir; here used to describe a concentrated "ferment" that can transform other substances.</note> ferment, without which nothing is ever perfected.

For just as dough cannot be leavened without a ferment <note>The author uses the analogy of bread-making (leavening) to explain how a small amount of "perfect" material can transform a larger mass of "imperfect" metal.</note>, so when you have sublimated the body, purified it, and separated the foulness from the dregs, and when you wish to join and mix them together, place the ferment in them. Prepare the watery earth until the <term>Ixsir</term> is fermented and becomes like dough.

son, our Lord Jesus Christ, and the Holy Spirit, the Comforter.
<margin>Concerning Christ</margin> Holy Trinity, you who are the only and one God, and perfect man, I give you thanks. Because when I had fully recognized the fleeting things of this world’s adversary, you lifted me up from that same world by your supreme mercy, so that I would not be enticed by its pleasures. But because I see countless people deceived in this art <term>art: specifically the alchemical "Great Art" (Ars Magna)</term>, who do not enter upon the direct path, may it please you, Lord my God, that through the knowledge which you have handed down to me, I might turn my dear friends away from this error; so that when they have perceived the truth, they may praise your holy and glorious name, which is blessed forever, Amen.

### PREFACE.

I, called Hortulanus <note>Latin for "The Gardener." This pseudonym belongs to a 14th-century alchemist, sometimes identified with Garlandius, whose commentary became one of the most influential interpretations of the Emerald Tablet.</note>, so named from the maritime gardens, wrapped in a Jacobin skin <note>original: "pelle Iacobina." This is a reference to the Dominican Order, known in Paris as "Jacobins" because their primary convent was located on the Rue Saint-Jacques.</note>, and unworthy to be called a disciple of philosophy—moved by love for my dear friend, I intend to set forth the most certain explanation of the Speech of Hermes, the father of philosophers. # THAT THE ART OF ALCHEMY
## *is true and certain.*

### CHAPTER I.

The philosopher <note>referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note> says: IT IS TRUE, namely, that the art of Alchemy has been given to us. WITHOUT FALSEHOOD. He says this to express his contempt for those who claim the art is deceitful, that is, false. CERTAIN, meaning it is proven by experience. For whatever has been experienced is most certain, AND MOST TRUE. For the most true Sun <term>Sun: in alchemical texts, "Sol" or the Sun almost always refers to gold, the most perfect of metals</term> is produced through this art. He uses the phrase "most true" in the superlative degree because the gold generated through this art surpasses all natural gold in all medicinal and other properties.

->COMMENTARY ON THE SEVENTH TREATISE<-

### THAT THE STONE MUST BE DI-
### vided into two parts.

### CHAPTER II.

Consequently, he touches upon the operation of the stone, saying: <term>THAT WHICH IS BELOW IS LIKE THAT WHICH IS ABOVE.</term> <note>original: "QVOD EST INFERIVS, EST SICVT QVOD EST SVPERIVS." This is perhaps the most famous line in alchemical literature, expressing the correspondence between the microcosm and macrocosm.</note> And he says this for this reason: That this stone is divided into two prin-
<margin>✓</margin> cipal parts through the mastery <note>original: "magisterium." This refers to the "Great Work" or the specialized process of alchemical transformation.</note>. Into an upper part, which ascends upward, and into a lower part, which remains below, fixed and clear. And yet these two parts agree in power. And therefore he says: <term>THAT WHICH IS ABOVE IS LIKE THAT WHICH IS BELOW.</term> However, that division is necessary <term>TO PERFORM THE MIRACLES OF THE ONE THING</term>, namely, the stone. Because the lower part is the earth, which is called the Nurse <note>original: "Nutrix." In alchemy, the "Nurse" is the earth or the vessel that nourishes the growing "philosopher's stone."</note> and the ferment. And the upper part is the soul, which vivifies and resuscitates the whole stone; and therefore, once the separation is made and the union <note>original: "coniunctione." The "Chemical Wedding" or the recombining of purified elements.</note> celebrated, many miracles are performed in the secret work of nature.

<meta>catchword: THAT</meta>

<summary>Hortulanus explains the Emerald Tablet's principle of "as above, so below" by describing the division of the alchemical stone into a volatile "soul" and a fixed "earth." He asserts that the union of these two separated parts is what enables the miracles of the Great Work.</summary>
<keywords>Emerald Tablet, Hermes Trismegistus, duality, Magisterium, Nutrix, soul, fermentation, separation, conjunction, alchemy</keywords>

->TREATISE OF HERMES<-

## THAT THE STONE CONTAINS WITHIN ITSELF THE FOUR ELEMENTS.

### CHAPTER III.

**AND JUST AS ALL THINGS CAME FROM THE ONE BY THE MEDITATION OF THE ONE.** <note>original: "ET SICVT OMNES RES FVERVNT AB VNO MEDITATIONE VNIVS." This is a foundational axiom of the Emerald Tablet, suggesting that the entire universe—and the alchemist's Work—springs from a single divine thought or source.</note> Here he gives an example, saying: Just as all things were from one—namely, from a "confused globe" or a "confused mass" <note>original: "massa confusa." This refers to the *Prima Materia* or "First Matter," the chaotic, undifferentiated substance that alchemists believe existed at the beginning of the world and must be rediscovered to begin the Great Work.</note>—by meditation, that is, by the thought and creation of the One, meaning Almighty God. **SO ALL THINGS WERE BORN**, that is, they emerged, **FROM THIS ONE THING**, namely, from one confused mass, **BY ADAPTATION**, that is, by the sole command and miracle of God. In the same way, our stone is born and has emerged from one confused mass containing all the elements within itself, which was created by God; and by His miracle alone, our stone is born from it.

# COMMENTARY ON THE SEVEN TREATISES
<meta>catchword: that</meta>

**IT IS HERE**, that is, within the work of the stone lies the final path. And take note: the Philosopher <note>original: "Philosophus." This refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of alchemy.</note> calls this operation the father of every **talisman** <note>original: "telesmi," from the Greek *telesmos*, meaning a completed rite or a perfected object; here it refers to the completed "Stone" or secret treasure.</note>, which is to say, of every secret or every treasure of the entire world—meaning every [philosopher's] stone found in this world. **IT IS HERE**. It is as if he says: "Behold, I show it to you." Afterward, the Philosopher says: "Do you wish for me to teach you when the strength of the stone is perfected and complete? It is when it shall have been turned into its own earth." And therefore he says: **ITS POWER IS INTEGRAL** <note>original: "VIS EIVS INTEGRA EST." *Integra* suggests something that is whole, undiminished, or restored to its perfect state.</note>, that is, perfected and complete, **IF IT BE TURNED INTO EARTH**. This means that if the soul of the stone itself—which was mentioned previously (where it was said the soul is the wind and air, containing the stone's entire life and strength)—should be turned into the "earth" of the stone and become **fixed** <note>original: "fixetur." Fixation is the alchemical process of making a volatile substance solid and resistant to fire.</note>. This is done so that the entire substance of the stone remains with its nurse, the earth, until the whole stone is turned into a **ferment** <note>original: "fermentum." In alchemy, a ferment is a substance that has the power to "infect" or transform a larger body into its own nature, much like yeast.</note>. Just as in the making of bread, a small amount of leaven nourishes and ferments a great quantity of dough, thereby converting the entire substance of the dough into leaven, so the Philosopher intends that our stone should be so fermented,

->ON THE PURIFICATION<-
->of the stone.<-

### CHAPTER VII.

Next, he sets forth how it ought to be multiplied. But first, he establishes the **purification of the stone** and the **separation of its parts**, saying: **YOU SHALL SEPARATE THE EARTH FROM THE FIRE, THE SUBTLE FROM THE GROSS, GENTLY WITH GREAT INGENUITY**. "Gently" means little by little, not through violent force, but with ingenuity—specifically, in **philosophical dung** <note>original: "fimo philosophico." This is a technical term for a source of gentle, consistent heat, such as a heat-retaining pit of decomposing manure or a specialized oven, used to mimic natural gestation.</note>. "You shall separate" means you shall **dissolve** <note>original: "dissolues." To the alchemist, dissolution is the first step in purification, breaking a solid down into its constituent parts.</note>, because dissolution is the separation of parts. "The earth from the fire, the subtle from the gross" refers to removing the **dregs** <note>original: "feces." The solid impurities or "filth" that remain after the pure essence has been extracted.</note> and impurities from the fire, air, and water, and from the entire substance of the stone, so that the stone may remain there in its most pure state, without any filth.

# COMMENTARY ON THE SEVENTH TRACTATE

...must be added to the fixed part for **multiplying**. And now he sets forth its multiplication, and its easy **liquefaction** <note>original: "liquefactionem." The process of making a solid substance flow like a liquid, often through heat or chemical change.</note> through its **ingressive power** <term>ingressive power: the "penetrating" quality of the alchemical medicine, allowing it to soak into and transform the interior of base metals.</term>, as much in hard bodies as in soft, saying: **IT ASCENDS FROM THE EARTH TO THE HEAVEN, AND AGAIN DESCENDS TO THE EARTH.**

It is highly important to note here that although **our stone** <note>original: "lapis noster." A traditional term for the substance that will become the Philosopher's Stone.</note> is divided into four parts in the first operation—which are the **four elements** <note>The classic elements of Earth, Air, Fire, and Water, which alchemists believed were the building blocks of all matter.</note>—nevertheless, as was stated above, it has two principal parts. One is that which ascends to the top, which is called **unfixed** <note>original: "non fixa." Also known as "volatile," referring to the spirit or gas that rises when heated.</note>, and the other remains below and is **fixed**. This latter is called **earth**, or **ferment**, which nourishes and ferments the entire stone, as has been said.

Indeed, one must obtain a great quantity of that unfixed part and give it to the stone (which has been made most pure and free from filth) through the **magistery** <term>magistery: the "Mastery" or specific technical process of the Great Work.</term> as many times as necessary, until the entire stone is carried upward by the power of the **spirit**, through **subliming** and **refining** <note>original: "sublimando & subtiliando." Sublimation involves heating a substance until it rises as vapor and collects at the top of a vessel, thereby "refining" it from its heavier, impure dregs.</note>. And this is what the philosopher says: **IT ASCENDS FROM THE EARTH TO THE HEAVEN.**

<meta>catchword: BECAUSE</meta>

<summary>This page explains the alchemical process of sublimation, where the 'unfixed' spirit of the stone is repeatedly combined with the 'fixed' earth to purify and multiply its power. This reflects the Emerald Tablet's cycle of ascending to heaven and descending to earth.</summary>

<header>COMMENTARY ON THE SEVENTH TRACTATE</header>

the philosopher <note>original: *philosophus* - referring to Hermes Trismegistus</note> taught the composition of the stone; here he teaches in a hidden way <note>original: *occulto modo* - a secret or veiled manner typical of alchemical writing</note> what our stone is made from. By naming himself beforehand, so that his disciples who reach this knowledge might perpetually remember his name, he then touches upon what it consists of, saying: **HAVING THREE PARTS OF THE PHILOSOPHY OF THE WHOLE WORLD.** Because whatever is in the world that possesses matter and form is composed of the **four elements**. Thus, the parts of the world are infinite, all of which the philosopher divides into three principal parts: namely, the Mineral, Vegetable, and Animal kingdoms. Concerning these—whether joined together or divided—the philosopher possessed true knowledge in the **Work of the Sun** <term>Work of the Sun: A traditional term for alchemy, specifically the process of creating gold or achieving the "Great Work."</term>. And therefore he says, "having three parts of the philosophy of the whole world," because these parts are contained within a single stone, namely the **Mercury of the Philosophers** <term>Mercury of the Philosophers: A secret, refined substance—not common mercury—considered the "root" or "spirit" of the Philosopher's Stone.</term>.

<header>THE COUNSEL OF MARRIAGE</header>

# HERE BEGINS THE BOOK
## OF THE COUNSEL OF MARRIAGE
### *concerning the mass of the Sun and Moon, with their summaries.* <note>original: *de massa Solis & Lunae* — in alchemy, the Sun and Moon represent Gold and Silver, or the active/male and passive/female principles that must be united.</note>

---

<image-desc>A square woodcut decorative initial 'N' containing a figure amidst scrolling foliage.</image-desc>

It is not read that a greater gift has emanated from divine munificence than wisdom, whence Hamuel <note>Hamuel is an authority often cited in medieval alchemical compendiums, likely an Arab philosopher or a legendary figure associated with Hermetic tradition</note> says: Entrust it to memory, adorn the conscience, and magnify knowledge. For he who scorns knowledge, the sublime and glorious God scorns him. However, the wise and chosen servants of God—of whom one after another has inherited this wisdom by the grace and gift of God (blessed be the name of the Lord)—so that they might create a perpetual memory for posterity, have written their books concerning this subject, veiled in **symbolic and metaphorical language** <note>original: *typicis & tropicis locutionibus* — referring to the "Decknamen" or code-names alchemists used to hide their secrets from the uninitiated</note>. For unless they had described this wisdom for future ages, these things would have perished, consigned to oblivion, just as their masters have died. For books are the **vessels of memory** and the perpetual fame of the wise.

The second reason is found in the *Book of Lights* by Rasis <note>original: *Rasis* — referring to Rhazes (Abu Bakr al-Razi), a famous 9th-century Persian physician and alchemist</note>. After he had taught many things concerning the arrangement of the art, he said: "Although all things have been set forth clearly, I nevertheless judge it proper that as many things as possible be wrapped in the form of riddles <note>original: *sub ænigmatis specie*</note>. For if I wished to explain all things exactly as they are, there would no longer be any place for prudence, since the foolish would be made equal to the wise; nor would any mortal under the solar or lunar circle <note>Refers to the sublunary world, or the realms governed by the Sun (gold) and Moon (silver)</note> bewail the straits of poverty as a stepmother." 

The third reason for the veiling of this science is recorded so that no occasion for evildoing be given to the wicked; this science is hidden in obscure words, though it ought not to be hidden from the good. Assiduus says: "Certain of the Philosophers, fearing the Lord, did not hold this mastery <term>mastery: *magisterium*, the successful completion of the alchemical Great Work</term> to be so sublime, nor so precious [that they wished to hide it]. <margin>openly</margin> 
open and manifest. ## COUNSEL ON THE MARRIAGE
<meta>catchword: through</meta>

...plainly something from them, my book would be just like that of the wise man himself, and my words would be his words, or theirs, and [the work] would be attributed to me. This is improper, and a disgrace to anyone who does so, because he seeks only vainglory.

Nevertheless, if I have labored greatly in this knowledge <note>original: *scientia*; the sacred art of alchemy</note>, and if their hidden figures <note>original: *tropi*; rhetorical devices or metaphors used to hide secrets</note> have been opened to me, and if that which they concealed has been made manifest to me—having grasped through science what they hid—then I ought to bring this closer to the understanding of my successors. I shall do so using "words openly veiled," signifying a hidden and shrouded meaning, so that the work may be simultaneously revealed and concealed.

For it is indeed open to the studious, the wise, the intelligent, and the seekers, but hidden from the foolish. If I did not act in this way, I would not demonstrate my own diligence <note>original: *industriam*; referring to his personal skill and hard-won expertise</note> above others, and my sayings would be like a proposition without a proof <note>original: *demonstratione*</note>—which is not how I have acted. And know that for him who perceives this knowledge in the right way, the words of my explanation shall not be hidden, especially for those into whose hands my books have come. And may God be my witness...

I shall set down a **Poem of three hundred verses**, and I shall provide a commentary on the sayings of the Philosophers, explaining to our community whatever they obscured to the best of their ability, and concealed from their fathers and sons. ### THE COUNSEL OF THE MARRIAGE

...ments, <note>original: *alumentis* or *alimentorum*; completing the sentence "source of all nourishment" from the previous page</note> the **Sun** is also the lord of bodies and stones, and is more noble than they, since it is their king and their abundance. Neither earth, nor water, nor fire destroys it, nor is it diminished in fire. Instead, fire rectifies and moistens it, and corrupting **sulphurs** <note>In alchemical theory, common sulphur was often seen as a "burning" impurity that caused metals to rust or decay; the "Sun" (Gold) is unique because it resists this "combustion."</note> do not burn it, because its nature is equal, clear, and rightly tempered. For this reason, the **Sages** <note>original: *Sapientes*; a common term for the "Wise" ancient philosophers and alchemists</note> extolled it and magnified it, and from it they established the composition of the **Elixir** <term>Elixir|A transformative substance, often used interchangeably with the Philosophers' Stone, capable of perfecting base metals or healing the human body</term> of the great stone; for it is a substance that is equal, permanent, and fixed through the length of eternity.

This **Elixir** is indeed of the same complexion as the substance of gold, because it is hot and moist <note>This refers to the four Aristotelian qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry). Gold and the Sun were traditionally classified as "hot and moist," which were the qualities associated with life and air.</note>. This Elixir is to physical bodies what the Sun is to the stars—for the Sun is the king of the stars and their light, and through it the things of the earth are completed, including plants, fruits, and minerals. Indeed, it retains every body, and is retained by them.

It is also the **ferment** <term>ferment (fermentum)|Just as yeast or "leaven" transforms dough into bread, alchemists believed a "ferment" of gold or silver was needed to "leaven" the alchemical work into its final, perfect state</term> of the two **Elixirs**, the white and the red <note>The "White Elixir" was used for transmuting metals into silver, while the "Red Elixir" was for gold.</note>. They are not rectified except through it, nor are they completed by any other thing—just as dough is not completed except through

->ferment-<-

# OF THE SUN AND MOON

...ferment <note>original: *fermentum*; completing the sentence from the previous page: "...and through it [the Sun] they established... a ferment."</note>. And this ferment is in the **Elixir**, just as rennet <note>original: *coagulum*; the substance used to curdle milk into cheese, used here as an analogy for how the Elixir "sets" or transforms the base metal.</note> is in milk, and like musk is in a good color <gloss>scent</gloss>. 

The **Moon** <note>The alchemical name for Silver</note> indeed is the spouse of the **Sun**, celestial in color, close to the Sun’s complexion <note>In medieval science, "complexion" refers to the specific balance of the four qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry.</note>, and she is the lady of moisture, from which all things are procreated with the help of the **Sun**. 

**Mercury**, moreover, is the lord of merchants, who discovers for merchants the gold, silver, and gems that wish to lie hidden in the mud. And **Mercury** is part of the matter of all metals. It is composed of an earthy and subtle dryness, and an unctuous <note>Oily or fatty; in alchemy, "unctuous moisture" is the essential "glue" that allows metals to be malleable and prevents them from being destroyed by heat.</note>, non-combustible moisture, possessing a most forceful and excellent universal complexion—except that it tends toward a watery moisture. Hence, by reason of this moistening moisture, it flows when cold, seeking a path elsewhere, and it flees from the fire. 

And by reason of its unctuous, non-combustible moisture and forceful complexion, it is not consumed by fire, but is entirely sublimated <term>Sublimation|The process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then collecting the purified deposit as it cools; in alchemy, this was a key method of purification.</term>. For the unctuous moisture, moved by the fire, draws its subtle dryness entirely with it, so that it leaves no dregs <note>original: *feces*; the solid impurities or "dross" left after a chemical reaction.</note> behind. By reason of its dryness, it flows in globules <note>original: *globose*; referring to how liquid mercury beads into spheres rather than spreading like water.</note>, and does not wet that which it touches, nor does it adhere by reason of...

<summary>This page describes the Sun as a catalyst (ferment) and the Moon as the "spouse" of the Sun and ruler of moisture. It provides a detailed analysis of Mercury’s unique physical properties, such as its ability to bead without wetting surfaces and its tendency to evaporate (flee) when heated.</summary>

-># OF THE SUN AND MOON<-

Through these things and by those which have been spoken of, the wise have prepared their stone from the best mineral <note>original: *minera*; refers to the raw ore or source material from which the alchemical stone is extracted.</note> they can find.

<margin>Geber was ignorant of the primary natural principles,</margin>
For this reason Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–813), a foundational figure in alchemy. Here, the author critiques him for focusing on technical processes while supposedly neglecting the underlying "natural principles."</note>, who was ignorant of the primary natural principles, is already far removed from our art, because he does not possess a true root upon which to fund his intention. He who truly knows the principles of nature, its causes, and its essences, yet has not yet achieved the true end and progress of this most hidden art, nevertheless possesses a more easy entrance. And such a man is but a little way removed from the doorway of the art. He who truly knows all the principles and causes of minerals, and the manner of their generation <note>the alchemical belief that metals "grow" like seeds within the earth.</note>—which consist of the intention of nature—very little indeed will be taken from him regarding the completion of the work, by which alone our science can be perfected.

<margin>the species of the art: the Sun <note>Gold</note>, the Moon <note>Silver</note>, stones, and gems</margin>
The **Philosophers' Stone** is a king descending from heaven, whose mountains are silver, and whose streams are golden, and whose earth is stones and precious gems. Behold, now all the species of the art have been enumerated. Diligently know, my son, that the work of the Philosophers and the Prophets is not made except in silver alone...

...with quicksilver <note>original: *argento vivo*; literally "living silver," the common name for mercury, which was considered the "soul" or "life-force" of all metals.</note>, since while they were pondering all things and arriving at the knowledge of quicksilver, they did not know which quicksilver they should take for their work. 

<margin><unclear>force</unclear></margin>
Therefore, they reconsidered their thoughts and their understanding of the various natures and accidental properties <note>In the philosophy of the time, "accidents" are external qualities—like color or smell—that can change without changing the essential nature of the substance.</note> which alter all things.

When, however, their focus was upon the stone, and they recognized that quicksilver proceeds from this stone—which some of them wished to conceal greatly—they called it by every possible name and likened it to every task and every thing. 

Some used only one name, which is "the stone," while the water that is extracted from it <note>The Latin text here uses masculine, feminine, and neuter pronouns for "it," emphasizing that the stone contains all genders and natures.</note> they called quicksilver. They divided this into two parts, which they found necessary to call by two different names. 

For some called them **heaven and earth**. all bronze is gold. <note>Continuing from the previous page: "all gold is bronze, but not all bronze is gold." This suggests a hierarchical relationship where gold contains the essence of bronze, but bronze must be perfected to become gold.</note> **Text.** But all oil is compared to the sun and to gold. Therefore **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus.</note> says that **fatness** <term>pinguedo; in alchemy, the "unctuous" or oily soul of a substance that allows it to catch fire or combine.</term> is from the dominion of the **Sun**. And **moisture** <note>original: *humiditas*</note> is from the dominion of the **Moon**, because the oil of everything cold is moisture, and the oil of everything hot is fatness. **Text.** And secondarily, regarding **death** <note>original: *mortem*; likely referring to the *mortificatio* stage, where the substance is broken down.</note>, oil is expressed from it, but on account of the dryness of the earth, after the entire vapor of the air has been extracted into a water similar to itself. > I say to you that iron is not made rusty, except by that water.

And it is this water which tinted the plates. And **Bonellus** <note>A speaker in the *Turba Philosophorum*, an influential early Latin alchemical text.</note> says:

> But I say to you: Take iron, making plates from it, afterwards sprinkle them with **venom** <term>venenum; often used to describe a corrosive acid or the transformative "mercurial" agent.</term>, and let them be placed in a vessel, the mouth of which is well closed, etc.

And because of the **blackness** appearing in the first and second work, it is called **Mars** <note>The planet and god associated with the metal iron.</note>. Wherefore **Florus** says:

> And know that the first blackness comes from the nature of **Mars**, and from that blackness arises **redness**, which purified the black, and ordained a peace that is both fleeing and not fleeing. <note>original: *pacem interfugiens, & non fugiens*; a paradoxical alchemical description of a stable yet volatile state.</note>

[the] elder <note>original: *senioris*; referring to Senior Zadith</note> says. There is a threefold **water of life** <term>aqua vitae; in alchemy, this is not alcohol, but a potent solvent or "mercurial water" capable of dissolving gold</term>, because it is one substance in which three reside: namely air, fire, and water. These are the three natures joined in the water, saying that they sought the natures of the **four elements** <note>original: *quatuor elementorum*</note>, namely in the earth. For in a dead body there are four: the **Hot, the Moist, the Cold, and the Dry**. 

These are united through that water which performs all works: it **vivifies** <note>gives life</note>, it **mortifies** <note>kills or breaks down</note> by **calcining** <term>calcination; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to achieve decomposition or oxidation</term>, it dissolves other things, and it **coagulates** <note>thickens or solidifies</note> itself with the earth. 

**Text.** These four natures the **ants**—that is, the **blackness** <term>nigredo; the "black work," the first stage of alchemy representing decay and the stripping away of old forms</term> from the belly of the earth of the **Philosophical Copper** <note>original: *æris Philosophici*; a symbolic base metal, not common copper</note>—first led out into the water. This water is neither hot, nor moist, nor cold, nor dry, but is an incorruptible medium of all, joining the **fixed tinctures** <note>permanent colors or medicinal qualities that cannot be destroyed by fire</note>.

# THE COUNSEL ON MARRIAGE
<note>original: *Consilium Conjugii*; a famous alchemical treatise often attributed to various authors, focusing on the "marriage" of solar and lunar principles.</note>

<margin>
Copper, gold,
and silver
are the same.
To burn and
to whiten
is the same.
</margin>

Burn the copper and the gold; burn the silver and burn the gold, for they are but one. Burn, burn! For to burn is to **whiten** <term>dealbare; the *albedo* stage of alchemy where the material is purified until it turns white</term>, specifically in the first and second whitening. And to make it **red** <note>original: *rubeum*; the *rubedo*, or final stage of the alchemical Great Work</note> is to **vivify** <note>to give life or restore spirit</note>, which is the end of the work. And the same author adds: The definition of this art is the liquefaction of the body—that is, its dissolution into water—and the separation of the soul from the body, as well as the reunion of the soul with its purified body. 

For our **copper** <note>original: *aes*; here referring to the base alchemical substance rather than common metal</note> has a soul and a body, just as a human does. It is therefore necessary to destroy the body and separate the soul from it; for it is not the body that penetrates, but the **soul** <term>anima; the volatile or "spiritual" essence of the substance</term>. That is to say, the **tincture** <term>tinctura; the concentrated essence capable of "dyeing" or transforming base metals into gold</term> in the water, which is subtle and dyeing, penetrates the body itself (which is the dead earth). Thus, the body and soul become one in nature. 

And he adds a beautiful and obscure riddle: that the **splendor of Saturn** <note>original: *splendor Saturni*; Saturn usually represents lead or the black stage of the work</note>—that is, the **solar blackness** <note>original: *nigredo solaris*; a paradox where the bright Sun/Gold is reduced to a black, decayed state</note>, which is its spirit and tincture—climbs into the air. This means it is resolved into an airy vapor within its own water, from which it originated. At first, it appears only as dark—that is, it thrives—and then the **offspring of the Philosophers** <note>the Philosopher's Stone</note> is born. 

**Text.** Mercury—that is, **Stilbon** <note>original: *Stilbon*; a Greek name for the planet Mercury, meaning "The Shining One"</note>, as if "dripping [a] good..." <meta>catchword: bonum</meta>

<summary>This page defines the alchemical process as a cycle of "burning" (purification), whitening, and reddening (completion). It uses the allegory of the soul being separated from and then reunited with a "dead" body to explain the dissolution and coagulation of matter.</summary>

<keywords>whitening, dealbatio, tincture, soul and body, Saturn, nigredo, solar blackness, Stilbon, mercury, dissolution</keywords>

# OF THE SUN AND MOON:
<note>original: *SOLIS ET LVNAE*; referring to the two primary principles of alchemy, the Solar (Gold/Sulfur) and the Lunar (Silver/Mercury)</note>

<margin>Stilbon</margin>
<note>Greek name for the planet Mercury, meaning "the glittering one." In this context, it refers to the alchemical spirit of mercury.</note>
...it meets the solar ray—that is, the splendor of the **Sun**—so that light may be joined to light, and it may be **whitened** after the greatest **blackness** <term>nigredo; the initial stage of decomposition and purification in the Great Work</term>. **The Text.** And **quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum vivum*</note>, which is the "living water," by its fiery virtue (that is, its solar power, which is more potent than actual fire), **vivifies** <note>gives life to</note> the dead body; that is, it makes it **red** <term>rubificatio; the *rubedo* or final stage where the material reaches its highest perfection</term>, and perfects it—that is, **fixes** it <term>fixatio; the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat</term>. 

Furthermore, concerning this **uneven stone**, Castus says in the *Assembly* <note>original: *Turba Philosophorum* (The Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the oldest and most influential Latin alchemical texts</note>: "If you wish to test our **coin**—that is, our **copper** <note>original: *aes*; in alchemy, this usually refers to the "philosophers' copper," a specialized starting material rather than common metal</note>—for its perfection, look to see if it is an impalpable water; that is, clean and purged of blackness." Specifically, you should see the **oil** floating on top like small fragments; when it is gathered together and converted into a **white water**, if it is found to be such, it is ready, for otherwise it will not **dye** <note>original: *tingit*; to "tinge" or transform a base metal into gold</note>. 

**Rasis.** <note>Muhammad ibn Zakariya al-Razi (c. 865–925 AD), a Persian physician and alchemist known in the West as Rhazes</note> The **spirit** has entry into the bodies only when they are laid out so clean and purged. And this is the primary root of this business. **It follows.** And know that if you take anything other than our copper and our water, and moisten it in our water, it will profit you nothing. But if you moisten our copper by our water, you shall find the truth. And he adds:

# ADVICE ON THE MARRIAGE
<note>original: *CONSILIVM CONIVGII*; the title of this influential alchemical treatise, which uses the metaphor of a "marriage" to describe the union of chemical principles.</note>
<meta>catchword: -jects (part of "subjects")</meta>

<margin>The coin-like stone, copper, and water.</margin>
By our **Copper** <note>original: *Aes*; in alchemy, this refers not to common metal, but to the "philosophical copper," a purified starting material</note> and our permanent water, the Philosophers signified in this stone—which they also commanded to be cooked over a slow fire—those two things which are indeed our **coin-like stone, namely copper and water.** And these are also the things of which the wise men said that *nature rejoices in nature* <note>a famous alchemical maxim attributed to the Greek alchemist Bolos of Mendes</note>, because of the closeness of nature within those two: that is, the male and the female, as well as our water. And it is of the same nature, namely the water of those two—that is, of the **Sun** 
<margin>☉ (Sun) & ☽ (Moon) and Water</margin>
and the **Moon**—between which the greatest kinship exists. If this kinship were not so immediate, they would not be converted so quickly, namely into one. And as Castus says in the *Assembly* <note>original: *Turba Philosophorum* (The Assembly of the Philosophers)</note>: "Nothing comes from a human except a human, nor from a winged creature except a winged creature." And nature is not corrected <note>original: *emendatur*; improved or perfected</note> except through nature. From this nature indeed, and from no other, the art is cultivated; unless you grasp and moisten it, you do not act well. Join, therefore, the 
<margin>☉ (Sun) ☽ (Moon) water</margin>
**male**, that is the **Sun** the son, to his **sister**, that is the **fragrant Moon**—fragrant by way of its scent or smoky evaporation—and they shall give birth to the art for you. Do not introduce any foreign powder to them, nor *any other thing, except their own water, and sub-*
<image-desc>A small manicule (pointing hand) in the left margin points toward the final instruction to use no foreign materials other than "their own water."</image-desc>

<summary>This page explains the "marriage" of the Sun (Gold/Sulfur) and Moon (Silver/Mercury), emphasizing that the alchemical process must rely on materials of the same "nature" rather than foreign substances. It invokes the famous maxim that "nature rejoices in nature" to justify why these specific substances bond into one.</summary>

<header>THE COUNSEL OF THE MARRIAGE</header>

Therefore, join quicksilver <note>original: *argentum vivum*; literally "living silver," our modern mercury</note> to quicksilver of its own kind; once this is finished, you will possess the greatest secret <note>original: *archanum*</note>.

Composite 
waters; 
coagulation

Magnesia

attracts iron,

Cook this in a light fire until the body is broken and becomes water, and cast in the **Ethelie** <note>An alchemical term for the volatile spirit or the "hidden" part of the matter.</note>, that is, join the **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum</term>, and roast them together with a slow fire until it becomes a broth, fattened by **solar grease** <note>original: *pinguedine solari*. **Pythagoras** says in the *Turba* <note>Referring to the *Turba Philosophorum* (Assembly of the Philosophers).</note>: It is the **sharpest vinegar** <note>original: *acetum acerrimum*. In alchemy, this is not household vinegar but a powerful acidic solvent capable of "spiritualizing" gold.</note> which causes gold to become pure spirit; once this is obtained, neither whiteness, nor blackness, nor rust remains. And when this vinegar is mixed with the body, it turns it into spirit. It also tints it with a **spiritual tincture** which can never be erased.

And the **Son of Azir** <note>likely an Arabic alchemical authority, such as Khalid ibn Yazid, often cited in Latin translations.</note> says: This vinegar burns the body and turns it into ash, and likewise whitens it; and if it is cooked until the blackness is thoroughly removed, it will become a stone and the **whitest coin**. # THE ADVICE ON THE MARRIAGE
-><note>original: *CONSILIVM CONIVGII*; a famous alchemical treatise whose title refers to the "Chemical Wedding," the union of masculine and feminine principles to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note><-

<page-num>90</page-num>

...[servi]ce <note>The text begins mid-word, likely completing "officio" (service/office) or "artificio" (craft) from the previous page.</note>, in the craft of this work is found to be false and useless; it signifies a prohibition against vain things, such as pearls, quicksilver, 
<margin>Quicksilver <note>original: *Arg. viv.* (Argentum vivum)</note> flees by itself, but remains when joined with the fixed <note>In alchemy, "fixed" substances are those that resist the heat of fire without evaporating, whereas "volatile" substances flee it.</note>.</margin>
sulfur, arsenic, and sal ammoniac <note>original: *sale armoniaco*; ammonium chloride, used in alchemy to prepare metals.</note>, which flee from the fire. How, therefore, shall anything good be expected from that which is not permanent? For a tincture <term>tinctura</term> <note>The "tincture" is the concentrated essence or medicine capable of transforming base metals into gold.</note> which is extracted from a bodily and combustible root is brought to nothing, just as its root is. For things are not changed [away] from how they emerge from their own root; this is seen in minerals, nor is it seen [otherwise] in the seeds of vegetables or the seeds of animals, *just as a chick is made from an egg* through various transmutations from the source where it has its root. And similarly, a human is made from seed <note>original: *spermate*; the male generative principle</note>, with no other ingredient entering in except for the menstrual blood, from which the seed is made, as if from a blood similar to itself, by which it is nourished. <note>This reflects the Aristotelian and Galenic view of biology, where the male seed provides the "form" and the female blood provides the "matter" for the growing child.</note>

<margin>Dung, urine, etc., contain nothing metallic.</margin>
But the voiding of dung, and urine, and blood, hair, and eggs, and similar things have no kinship with metallic things. Thus, gold and silver cannot be made from their root, as some ignorant people think; for nothing comes out of a human except a human, nor from a like thing except what is like itself.

<meta>catchword: Therefore <note>original: *Igitur*</note></meta>

<summary>This page warns the alchemist against using volatile or purely organic substances like urine or hair, arguing that the "Stone" must be made from materials that share a fundamental "root" or nature with metals.</summary>

# OF THE SUN AND MOON.
-><note>original: *SOLIS ET LVNAE*; in alchemical code, the Sun represents Gold and the Moon represents Silver.</note><-

<page-num>91</page-num>

<margin>
And by a 
very similar [thing]
</margin>
Therefore, gold and silver [are produced] from nothing except from a species very like themselves. Whence says Senior <note>Zadith ben Hamuel, a 10th-century Arabic alchemist whose works were translated into Latin and became foundational to Western alchemy.</note>, the son of Hamuel: "Every living creature generates nothing from itself except with that which is from it—that is, with its own species, which is homogeneous <term>homogeneum</term> <note>Meaning "of the same kind." The author argues that just as humans only produce humans, gold can only be produced from a "seed" or "root" that is already metallic in nature.</note> to it." Similarly [it is with] Adam and Eve and the whole human race. Therefore, every thing agrees with its like and with what is near to it according to its species. 

Begin in the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, and know its nature; for it is from the root of its own matter, which is in the same and from the same, nor does any other thing enter into it that is not also from it, and which is its root. Similarly Mercury <note>original: *Mercurius*; here referring to "Philosophical Mercury," a specialized substance alchemists believed was the hidden heart of all metals.</note> is from it, and this is that from which it is extracted, and it is in it, and from it.

<image-desc>A vertical, hand-drawn wavy line in the right margin, likely a reader's mark or placeholder.</image-desc>

For the Philosopher’s Stone <term>Lapis Philosophorum</term> is a congealed water—namely, in gold and silver—and it resists the fire, and it is resolved back into its own water, from which it is composed according to its own kind. Whence Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary, mythical founder of the alchemical tradition.</note> [says]: "Nothing suits a thing except that which is closer to it by its own nature, and a progeny similar to it will be generated therein." And immediately he added: "the moisture, however, is from the dominion of the Moon, and the fatness..."

The Sun with two rays is the image of the divine water, in which there are three things: namely, fire, air, and water. This water is composed of two natures: male and female. And yet, it is a triple water because of a third thing added to the two. This water is not imbibed <note>original: *imbibitur*; the process where a solid "drinks" or absorbs a liquid essence.</note>—that is, ground down—all at once, but is individually imbibed through different rays, ground down, coagulated, and reduced to ashes <term>incineratur</term>. 

The Sun with only one ray signifies that the triple water is a single, homogeneous nature in one water. And although there are two works—the white <term>albedo</term> and the red <term>rubedo</term> <note>The two main stages of the Alchemical Great Work: the "White Work" creates silver, and the "Red Work" creates gold.</note>—nevertheless it is one single work, beginning in the same way and ending in the same way (and in no other way); it is the figure of "one in one." 

The Earth, indeed, is the lower body composed of two bodies, to which a third of its own kind is mixed. <page-num>105</page-num>
<header>OF THE SUN AND MOON.</header>

...you will perfect <note>Completing the word "perficietis" from the previous page.</note> the secret <term>archanum</term>, which the Philosophers have hidden in their books. The earth, however, which the black sphere surrounded, is the second blackness <term>nigredo</term> <note>The *nigredo*, or blackening, is the first major stage of the alchemical Great Work, symbolizing the death or decomposition of the matter.</note>, which comes from the moisture of the water poured upon the earth. But because it is divided into its third—that is, into three thirds—it signifies three preparations <term>falsaturas</term> <note>original: *falsaturas*; in this context, it refers to the shifting appearances or stages of the substance as it is refined.</note>, or three earths: namely, the earth of pearls, the earth of leaves, and the earth of gold. 

The earth of pearls is the first: the earth from which the tinctures are extracted in their own water, from which the pearls proceed. The second is the earth of leaves—that is, of colors—for it produces variable <note>original: *variabiles*.</note> colors until it is perfectly whitened; this is the third stage. 

Or, it can be said that the first is the earth of leaves, because the leaves of copper <term>folia aeris</term>, having been changed, pass into water and earth. The second is the earth of pearls, when it is fully whitened <term>dealbatur</term>. And the third is the earth of gold, which is the solar tincture; and that earth itself, whitened after the second blackening, is called the "Gold of the Philosophers" <term>aurum Philosophorum</term> <note>A term for the perfected substance of the Stone, which is not common gold but a spiritual or refined physical essence.</note>. And the water is their gold, because it is the mother of gold, since it generates gold according to the Elder <note>original: *Seniorem*; referring to Senior Zadith (Zadith ben Hamuel), a famous 10th-century Arab alchemist whose works were highly influential in the Latin West.</note>. And those three earths are one nature of one sphere and of one kind, and similarly it is un...

-># ADVICE ON THE MARRIAGE<-

It is to be understood as three preparations <term>falsaturas</term> <note>original: *falsaturis*; here referring to the chemical refinements or alterations of the substance.</note>, and soakings <term>imbibitionibus</term> <note>The process of adding liquid to a dry substance until it is saturated.</note>; with three-thirds of nine parts. The first third is congealed, and having been reduced to ash <term>incinerata</term>, is made masculine, and there are two: the Masculine of the Sun <note>Gold</note>, and of Mercury, and one female of a Lunar nature <note>Silver</note>. 

Then, through the remainder of the water, which are the six parts of colors, another female is made, and there will be two males and two females. But the second third is the preparation <term>falsatura</term>, which in its entirety is nothing other than saying: **calcine, dissolve, and distill, coagulate and reduce to ash**, and calcine again until the whole is coagulated, and finally fixed—which is the final third, and the end of the work. 

Regarding these three thirds, in the *Assembly of the Philosophers* <note>original: *Turba*; referring to the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest and most influential Latin alchemical texts.</note> in the chapter on riddles <term>ænigmatibus</term>, he divides his work into three through all the riddles. First, into the dead body <term>corpus mortuum</term> and the extraction of the soul from the body, which is the first work. Second, concerning the calcination <note>Reducing a substance to powder by heat.</note> of the body and its purification <term>mundificatione</term>, by soaking it in its own water and roasting it, until it is whitened in the manner of snow, or white salt. The third is the joining <term>sociatio</term> of the pure soul to the pure and white body, on account of which—

Whence Florinus says in the *Assembly*: "When you see that pre-eminent whiteness, be certain that redness is hidden within it, and then it behooves you to extract it, and [cook it] for so long..."

...to arrive at knowledge. This knowledge, however, is in a certain place, which place is indeed the four elements; and there are four gates. If you wish to know them, I say first that there are four stations, four corners, four boundaries, and four walls. I will explain these four keys at the end of this poem. 

It follows: This is the **house of treasures** <term>thesauraria</term> <note>original: *domus thesauraria*; a common alchemical metaphor for the hidden laboratory of Nature or the completed Philosopher's Stone which contains all "riches" or virtues.</note>, in which all things are stored as treasures—sublime matters of the sciences or wisdom, or the most glorious things, which cannot be possessed unless they are treasured in this house. 

The house in which these treasures are kept is closed by four gates, which are opened by four keys. There is one key for each of these gates. No one can enter this house, nor take anything from it, nor know the secret <term>archanum</term> <note>original: *archanum*; the "arcanum" is the inner mystery or the potent, transformed essence of a substance.</note> which is enclosed within it, unless he first knows the key and has it with him, or at least belongs to the household of the house.

**Of the Sun.** Its signs are these: namely, an abundant blackness <term>nigredo</term> <note>original: *nigredo*; the "blackening" is the first stage of the alchemical Great Work, signifying the decay or "death" of the starting material required for its eventual purification.</note>, which, when consumed, means the soul <term>anima</term> <note>original: *anima*; in alchemy, the "soul" refers to the vital, volatile essence or "oil" extracted from a substance.</note> has already been purified in the water through distillation. Victimerus <note>Victimerus is an obscure alchemical authority often cited in medieval compilations like the *Rosarium Philosophorum*.</note> says: It is beneficial for you to raise the water as vapor through sublimation. The **second key** is the thinning of the earthly body, since stiffness, flexibility, and impurity are within it, which cannot be pulled out except by laborious and subtle ingenuity, as is said in the *Assembly of Philosophers* <note>original: *Turba*; referring to the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts, written as a debate between Greek sages.</note>. For the body cannot be thinned as long as flexibility, stiffness, and hardness remain within it. ...of the elder. Next, I will briefly explain the doctrine of Affiduus <note>Likely a reference to Adfar, the legendary master of Morienus.</note>. ->**OF THE SUN AND MOON**<-

<margin>Fire of beasts.</margin>
...[not] of wood, but a subtle, well-filtered **fire of beasts** <note>original: *ignis bestiarum*; this refers to the gentle, steady heat produced by biological sources like fermenting horse manure, which alchemists used for the long-term "digestion" of materials.</note>. Bring the fire closer—that is, let it be stronger through the kneading of the earth with water—and by placing it nearby, preserve the putrefaction <term>putrefactio</term> <note>The stage where matter decays or breaks down, traditionally associated with the color black and the "death" of the substance before its rebirth.</note>. Gratianus says: "And let each calcined thing be dissolved in its own way, so that it may putrefy." And Morienus, in the dialogue by the Wise author, says that the entire strength of this mastery <term>magisterium</term> <note>The "Great Work" or the specialized process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> does not exist, except after putrefaction. For he says: "if it has not been putrefied, it will return to nothing." And he adds that after putrefaction, the Most High God will bring the desired composition to success. Forbid coldness and excessive heating—that is, avoid the excess of cold and heat, which is intemperance—that is, persevere in the middle path between extremes of heat, namely, a heat that is not excessive. And forbid every work that is beyond measure—that is, with excess and abundance—
<margin>The mass should be neither too dry nor too moist.</margin>
so that the mass <term>massa</term> <note>The physical lump of alchemical material.</note> is not too dry, nor too moist. Whence Bonellus says in the *Assembly of Philosophers* <note>original: *Turba Philosophorum*; one of the most influential early alchemical texts.</note>: "Know that if you increase the water in the mass, the fire will not contain it, nor will it burn well in the furnace. And if you dry it too much, it will not be joined together, nor will it be cooked." Do not hasten; as Rasis <note>The Latinized name for Al-Razi, a famous 9th-century Persian physician and alchemist.</note> says in...

...on the earth, do not allow it to be defiled <note>original: *foetifare*; to make foul or defiled.</note>, that is, cut away the impure things, namely, the dust or the blackness. However, the vivid colors are white, yellow <note>original: *citrinus*; citron-colored or golden yellow.</note>, and red, because as it is said in the *Assembly of Philosophers* <note>original: *Turba Philosophorum*; one of the most influential and oldest Latin alchemical texts, written as a transcript of a meeting of ancient sages.</note>: To burn is to <term>whiten</term> <note>original: *dealbare*; the *albedo* stage, which follows the blackening.</note> and to redden; and therefore he spoke of "animals" whose soul colors and brings life to the body.

Some books, however, have this: "And do not be reluctant to bring forth life" <note>original: *foetifare*; here the author notes a variant reading in other manuscripts where the word implies bearing offspring or "fruiting."</note>, that is, to produce; for the offspring of the Stone is a color that is always vivid and remains forever. 

Certain books say: "Do not exhaust the living things," that is, do not open [the vessel], for the vessel must be closed. But in the record <note>original: *registro*; likely referring to a table of signs or an experimental log.</note> you will be able to experience those "animals"—that is, the colors.
